---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final Response Citations

- **Create/edit:** for created or edited PDFs, cite each final deliverable exactly once with `purpose="output"`, in its own paragraph. Briefly describe the result without repeating its filename, path, or link. Never put output directives inside prose or list items.
- **Q&A and summaries:** Every file whose contents you summarize or use to support an answer MUST receive a `:codex-file-citation{...}` with `purpose="source"` in the final response. Use the source citation in place of the filename, wherever the filename would appear. Do not write the filename separately and append its citation at the end. Files merely inspected but not used in the answer need no citation. Do not emit output citations for unchanged files or read-only answers.

- Emit `:codex-file-citation{...}` directly, without backticks, code fences, or a Markdown-link wrapper. Use verified targets only. No need to cite or reference intermediate work.

For file citations, set `path` to the verified absolute file path on the task host. Omit `mode`.

Some illustrative examples, real responses use verified targets outside code fences.

```text
:codex-file-citation{path="/absolute/path/review.pdf" purpose="output"}
```

```text
The review of :codex-file-citation{path="/absolute/path/review.pdf" purpose="source"} is due Friday.
```


### Additional Citation Guidance

For PDF Q&A, inspect complete relevant pages and preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes. PDF citations support only plain file citations; do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators.

def mdtext(t):
 t=re.sub(r'<link href="([^"]+)">(.+?)</link>',lambda m:'['+m[2]+']('+m[1].replace('(','%28').replace(')','%29')+')',t)

\input{milestone_visual.tex}
\usepackage{graphicx}
\hypersetup{pdftitle={DS675 Mini-Project Milestone 2 - Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}}
\begin{document}\small
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{1/5}
\sectiontitle{1. Description of Dataset and Learning Task}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{1.1 Source, records and suitability}\par\vspace{.7mm}
BattLeDIM is an open, simulated drinking-water benchmark representing L-Town, inspired by Cyprus. It was created to compare leak detection/localization methods. Its continuous time series also allow a supervised pressure-forecasting task. Zenodo supplies supervisory control and data acquisition (SCADA) records, leak labels, sensor configuration and EPANET hydraulic models [\hyperlink{ref:1}{1},\hyperlink{ref:2}{2}]. These are simulated observations, not field measurements.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
The 2018-2019 release has five-minute spacing: 105,120 timestamps per year, 210,240 total, and 119 numerical measurement channels per timestamp. The nominal network contains 782 demand junctions, two reservoirs, one tank, 905 pipes, one pump and three pressure-reducing valves (PRVs). Nominal and generator parameters differ [\hyperlink{ref:1}{1}].\par\vspace{1.7mm}
{\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\hsize=0.51190\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=0.66667\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.82143\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\rowcolor{Blue!13} \textbf{Quantity} & \textbf{Channels / units} & \textbf{Meaning} \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}Pressure} & 33; m water head & Pressure at measured junctions. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}Pipe/pump flow} & 3; mÂ³/h & PUMP\_1 and p227/p235 flow. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}Tank level} & 1; m & Water level in T1. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}AMR demand} & 82; L/h & Automated meter reading (AMR): consumption assigned to junctions, not pipe inflow. \\
\end{tabularx}\par}
\vspace{1.7mm}
Our checks of all eight annual SCADA tables found matching, unique timestamps and no missing values. The benchmark assumes error-free sensors and no transmission delay/loss. Values are rounded to two decimals; pressure readings average the preceding five minutes [\hyperlink{ref:2}{2}], Sensors and Telemetry. Nearby time records are related, so the later evaluation keeps time order. Leak labels are not model inputs.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{1.2 Learning task and hydraulic feature selection}\par\vspace{.7mm}
The task is single-output supervised regression: y(u)=p\_n1(u+15 min), three samples ahead, in metres. The label is the recorded five-minute average ending at u+15, including the simulated operating/leak conditions. Each input row is paired with n1 three timestamps later; there are no target classes. The intended use is a short look-ahead for operators reviewing pressure changes at a remote junction; operational benefits are not evaluated here. Every input must be available at forecast time u.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=2.15in,keepaspectratio]{hydraulic_feature_scope.png}\end{center}{\footnotesize Figure 1. Functional supply boundary derived from the released model [\hyperlink{ref:1}{1}]. Nominal PRV settings are 40/50/35 m; constant settings do not fix remote-node pressures.\par}\vspace{1.8mm}
Area C is supplied through T1 and has 92 junctions, three pressure sensors and 82 AMRs. Our nominal-model checks give n1 the longest T1-to-sensor pipe path: 891 m, versus 574/308 m for n4/n31; this distance is not a signal delay. We keep n1/n4/n31 pressure, T1 level, PUMP\_1 flow and AMR\_n1 demand: 119 channels â†’ 87 local/boundary candidates â†’ six signals. T1 represents storage, PUMP\_1 measured inflow, n4/n31 the local pressure state, and AMR\_n1 consumption at the target junction. Unlike PRV-3's single measured pressure, C provides several related measurements. Whether they help n1 forecasting is a question to test.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\newpage
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{2/5}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{1.3 Own exploratory analysis}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Our exploratory data analysis (EDA) uses January-September 2018 only: 273 days and 78,624 timestamps. n1 ranges from 25.67 to 29.36 m. The generator opens PUMP\_1 below T1=2.4 m and closes it above 3.9 m [\hyperlink{ref:1}{1}]. Figure 2 shows how pressure, tank level and pump operation vary through the day; the mean curve does not describe every individual day.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=2.6in,keepaspectratio]{review_profiles_v011.png}\end{center}{\footnotesize Figure 2. Own EDA: T1/PUMP\_1 at left; n1/n4/n31 at right. Thin lines: individual days; red: clock-time mean.\par}\vspace{1.8mm}
For 78,621 valid +15 pairs, excluding the final three origins, persistence predicts the current n1 value. Its mean absolute error (MAE) is 0.0518 m (about 5.18 cm); root mean squared error (RMSE) is 0.0685 m and gives more weight to large errors. These are our 2018 descriptive results, not final test scores. Correlation with future n1 is 0.9923 for current n1, 0.9230 for T1, -0.0367 for PUMP\_1 and -0.1574 for AMR\_n1. Pressure changes slowly over 15 minutes, making persistence a strong baseline; high correlation alone does not show that an extra signal improves a forecast.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=2.05in,keepaspectratio]{review_correlations_v011.png}\end{center}{\footnotesize Figure 3. Own EDA: six origin inputs and the future label. The label is separated for analysis and never enters X.\par}\vspace{1.8mm}
\sectiontitle{2. Contributions of Previous Authors}
We searched BattLeDIM/L-Town predictive models and followed citations/code. Related papers/repositories form 35 groups of related works on L-Town or explicit modifications; external networks, reviews and tools were excluded. The five categories mean leak detection/location, current-pressure estimation, future-pressure/head forecasting, anomaly/sensor-fault detection, and sensor placement. This inventory is not exhaustive.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\newpage
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{3/5}
\begin{center}\includegraphics[width=\linewidth,height=1.4in,keepaspectratio]{review_selection_v022.png}\end{center}{\footnotesize Figure 4. Own screening: percentages use 35 groups; gold identifies six selected subsets. Primary diagnostic purpose can include an intermediate future-pressure prediction.\par}\vspace{1.8mm}
ChebNet, SAM-kNN and AGN are core future-state studies; LILA/GATRes are current-state comparators; EILF is supporting hydraulic engineering. SAM-kNN/AGN/GATRes generate new L-Town data rather than using the released SCADA.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
{\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\hsize=0.59524\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.26190\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.14286\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\rowcolor{Blue!13} \textbf{Study / task / code} & \textbf{Data preparation and how the method works} & \textbf{Reported results and what they show} \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}ChebNet [\hyperlink{ref:3}{3}]
+5-min pressure and leakage diagnosis. Code: [\hyperlink{ref:4}{4}].} & \textbf{Data:} Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and three past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split. \textbf{Models:} Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts healthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current pressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over seven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were compared. Code fits scaling before the split. & \textbf{2019 diagnosis, Table 1:} 19 accepted matches, 138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61\%, EUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300 m while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain; these results do not measure forecast-pressure error. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}SAM-kNN [\hyperlink{ref:5}{5}]
+5-min virtual pressure; anomalies. Code: [\hyperlink{ref:6}{6}]; scenarios not shared.} & \textbf{Data:} Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29 sensors). Code averages three past readings from other sensors to predict the target's next reading (+5 min); paper describes averaging four. \textbf{Model:} Adaptive nearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples and selects the memory with the lowest recent prediction error. & \textbf{Five leak cases, Table 1:} Each leak is detected, with 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus 11,146-19,216 for ordinary online kNN. These are alarm counts, not pressure errors. The scenarios are newly simulated, not the released 2018-2019 series. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}AGN [\hyperlink{ref:7}{7}]
Multistep future nodal heads. Code: no repository linked; data on request.} & \textbf{Data:} One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min samples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10 train/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use analysis guides features. \textbf{Model:} Graph links share information; recurrent units retain history; attention weights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head + elevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs. & \textbf{Future heads, Table 1:} 12/24/48/96-step outputs reach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window, regional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium), 32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole windows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate n1(+15) score is reported. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}LILA [\hyperlink{ref:8}{8}]
Current healthy-pressure reference; leaks. Code: [\hyperlink{ref:9}{9}].} & \textbf{Data:} Released SCADA; analyze pressure/flow relationships to select reference sensors and healthy periods. \textbf{Model:} Linear regression estimates current healthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds persistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic calculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw PUMP\_1. It is not a future forecast. & \textbf{Journal Table 1:} 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited leaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision 89.5\%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations outside 300 m count as false positives, not missed events; recall=1 therefore does not mean perfect localization. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}GATRes [\hyperlink{ref:10}{10}]
Current sparse-to-full pressure. Code: [\hyperlink{ref:11}{11}].} & \textbf{Data:} 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20 train/validation/test split); compare pressure distributions with time-series test data. \textbf{Model:} Hide selected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph attention weights neighboring information to reconstruct the hidden pressures at the same time point. & \textbf{Table 10 (text: 1 week; caption: 24 h):} Across ten training runs, MAE=0.9501Â±0.0086 m versus 1.5928Â±0.0050 m for the mGCN graph baseline. The Â± is the standard deviation between runs. This measures current-pressure reconstruction, not +15-min forecasting. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}EILF [\hyperlink{ref:12}{12}]
Engineering diagnosis, not future learning. Code: on request.} & \textbf{Data:} 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector outflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min windows to reduce level-rounding effects. \textbf{Method:} Separate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL), detect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically. & \textbf{Journal conclusion, p. 11:} TPR=47.83\%, five false positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak diagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The tank-balance calculation is an existing engineering contribution relevant to our feature selection. \\
\end{tabularx}\par}
\vspace{1.7mm}
TP/FP/FN count accepted matches, false alarms/wrong matches and missed leaks; TPR=recall; F1 combines precision/recall. EUR is the competition monetary score [\hyperlink{ref:2}{2}]. Graph filters, adaptive memories and CUSUM extend class baselines [\hyperlink{ref:4}{4},\hyperlink{ref:6}{6},\hyperlink{ref:9}{9}]. Training was not reproduced.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\textbf{Research gap.} Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value. This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\newpage
\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{4/5}
\sectiontitle{3. Your Anticipated Contributions}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work}\par\vspace{.7mm}
We propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [\hyperlink{ref:13}{13},\hyperlink{ref:14}{14}]; Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [\hyperlink{ref:15}{15}], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of worldwide priority.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
{\footnotesize\setlength{\tabcolsep}{4pt}\renewcommand{\arraystretch}{1.16}
\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}
\begin{tabularx}{\linewidth}{@{}>{\hsize=0.57540\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X>{\hsize=1.42460\hsize\raggedright\arraybackslash}X@{}}
\rowcolor{Blue!13} \textbf{Planned output} & \textbf{What we expect to learn} \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C1. Forecast-ready table} & Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C2. Boosted-tree forecast} & Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. \\
\textbf{\color{Navy}C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment} & Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history. \\
\end{tabularx}\par}
\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Use 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that all six signals help.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\[\widehat p_{n1}(u+15)=F_M(X(u)),\qquad F_m(X)=F_{m-1}(X)+\eta h_m(X).\]
Boosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each correction [\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7. Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows [\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. Boosted trees can capture nonlinear relationships among pressure, tank storage, pump operation and local consumption; an advantage over these baselines must be tested.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Initial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages \{100,200,300\} Ã— learning rates \{0.03,0.1\}, depth=2, minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max\_features=1.0, depth \{6,10,14\} Ã— minimum leaf \{10,40\}. Ridge alpha: \{0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000\}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set, chosen by validation MAE [\hyperlink{ref:16}{16},\hyperlink{ref:17}{17},\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
\vspace{1.5mm}\labeltext{3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement}\par\vspace{.7mm}
Train January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the 2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1, PUMP\_1, n4/n31 or AMR\_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.\par\vspace{1.7mm}
Recompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE\_model/MAE\_persistence (positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Limited or absent auxiliary-signal value is also informative: it supports a simpler model. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these comparisons do not prove statistical significance. Deliver the preparation/model notebooks, settings, score table and prediction/error plots. No learning model has been fitted. Delays/ages, balance proxies and block intervals remain outside this scope.\par\vspace{1.7mm}

Own screening: percentages use 35 groups; gold identifies six selected subsets. Primary diagnostic purpose can include an intermediate future-pressure prediction.\\\\par}\\\\vspace{1.8mm}\\r\\nChebNet, SAM-kNN and AGN are core future-state studies; LILA/GATRes are current-state comparators; EILF is supporting hydraulic engineering. SAM-kNN/AGN/GATRes generate new L-Town data rather than using the released SCADA.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n{\\\\footnotesize\\\\setlength{\\\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\\\\renewcommand{\\\\arraystretch}{1.16}\\r\\n\\\\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}\\r\\n\\\\begin{tabularx}{\\\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\\\hsize=0.59524\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X>{\\\\hsize=1.26190\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X>{\\\\hsize=1.14286\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X@{}}\\r\\n\\\\rowcolor{Blue!13} \\\\textbf{Study / task / code} & \\\\textbf{Data preparation and how the method works} & \\\\textbf{Reported results and what they show} \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}ChebNet [\\\\hyperlink{ref:3}{3}]\\r\\n+5-min pressure and leakage diagnosis. Code: [\\\\hyperlink{ref:4}{4}].} & \\\\textbf{Data:} Leak-free nominal simulations, network links and three past pressure readings; 80/20 train/validation split. \\\\textbf{Models:} Two four-layer graph networks: one forecasts healthy pressure (+5 min), the other reconstructs current pressure from available sensors. Their discrepancy over seven days helps locate leaks. Graph/scaling options were compared. Code fits scaling before the split. & \\\\textbf{2019 diagnosis, Table 1:} 19 accepted matches, 138 false alarms, four missed leaks; recall 82.61\\\\%, EUR326,521. A match requires a location within 300 m while the leak is active. Many false alarms remain; these results do not measure forecast-pressure error. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}SAM-kNN [\\\\hyperlink{ref:5}{5}]\\r\\n+5-min virtual pressure; anomalies. Code: [\\\\hyperlink{ref:6}{6}]; scenarios not shared.} & \\\\textbf{Data:} Ten new Area A simulations (3 months each; 29 sensors). Code averages three past readings from other sensors to predict the target's next reading (+5 min); paper describes averaging four. \\\\textbf{Model:} Adaptive nearest-neighbor regression stores recent/older examples and selects the memory with the lowest recent prediction error. & \\\\textbf{Five leak cases, Table 1:} Each leak is detected, with 3-48 false alarms per scenario versus 11,146-19,216 for ordinary online kNN. These are alarm counts, not pressure errors. The scenarios are newly simulated, not the released 2018-2019 series. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}AGN [\\\\hyperlink{ref:7}{7}]\\r\\nMultistep future nodal heads. Code: no repository linked; data on request.} & \\\\textbf{Data:} One generated year on modified L-Town: 5-min samples, 104 demand patterns; 70/20/10 train/validation/test split. Elevation and water-use analysis guides features. \\\\textbf{Model:} Graph links share information; recurrent units retain history; attention weights nodes/features to forecast head (pressure head + elevation). Training progressively hides fewer inputs. & \\\\textbf{Future heads, Table 1:} 12/24/48/96-step outputs reach +1/+2/+4/+8 h. For the 12-step window, regional MAE is 0.859 m (high), 0.327 m (medium), 32.7 m (low elevation). Errors average whole windows/regions; +15 is included, but no separate n1(+15) score is reported. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}LILA [\\\\hyperlink{ref:8}{8}]\\r\\nCurrent healthy-pressure reference; leaks. Code: [\\\\hyperlink{ref:9}{9}].} & \\\\textbf{Data:} Released SCADA; analyze pressure/flow relationships to select reference sensors and healthy periods. \\\\textbf{Model:} Linear regression estimates current healthy pressure from peer pressures/flow. CUSUM adds persistent deviations to detect leaks, then hydraulic calculations locate them. Paper: squared flow; code: raw PUMP\\\\_1. It is not a future forecast. & \\\\textbf{Journal Table 1:} 19 new 2019 leaks; four inherited leaks excluded. TP=17, FP=2, FN=0; precision 89.5\\\\%, F1=0.944, EUR307,852. Two locations outside 300 m count as false positives, not missed events; recall=1 therefore does not mean perfect localization. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}GATRes [\\\\hyperlink{ref:10}{10}]\\r\\nCurrent sparse-to-full pressure. Code: [\\\\hyperlink{ref:11}{11}].} & \\\\textbf{Data:} 10,000 simulated L-Town snapshots (60/20/20 train/validation/test split); compare pressure distributions with time-series test data. \\\\textbf{Model:} Hide selected readings and fill hidden inputs with zero. Graph attention weights neighboring information to reconstruct the hidden pressures at the same time point. & \\\\textbf{Table 10 (text: 1 week; caption: 24 h):} Across ten training runs, MAE=0.9501Â±0.0086 m versus 1.5928Â±0.0050 m for the mGCN graph baseline. The Â± is the standard deviation between runs. This measures current-pressure reconstruction, not +15-min forecasting. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}EILF [\\\\hyperlink{ref:12}{12}]\\r\\nEngineering diagnosis, not future learning. Code: on request.} & \\\\textbf{Data:} 2018 development/2019 test. In Area C, pump inflow minus the tank-storage change rate estimates sector outflow; summed AMRs provide a reference. Use 30-min windows to reduce level-rounding effects. \\\\textbf{Method:} Separate pressure trends from recurring cycles (STL), detect sustained changes, then locate leaks hydraulically. & \\\\textbf{Journal conclusion, p. 11:} TPR=47.83\\\\%, five false positives, EUR167,981. These measure leak diagnosis, not future-pressure accuracy. The tank-balance calculation is an existing engineering contribution relevant to our feature selection. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\end{tabularx}\\\\par}\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\nTP/FP/FN count accepted matches, false alarms/wrong matches and missed leaks; TPR=recall; F1 combines precision/recall. EUR is the competition monetary score [\\\\hyperlink{ref:2}{2}]. Graph filters, adaptive memories and CUSUM extend class baselines [\\\\hyperlink{ref:4}{4},\\\\hyperlink{ref:6}{6},\\\\hyperlink{ref:9}{9}]. Training was not reproduced.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\textbf{Research gap.} Among the reviewed studies, none reports a directly comparable n1(+15) forecast evaluation on the released SCADA. Leak diagnosis, current reconstruction and forecasts with other data/horizons answer different questions. We therefore compare Gradient Boosting, persistence, Ridge and Random Forest on common chronological splits and test auxiliary-signal value. This provides reproducible classical baselines for future comparison with complex models, which are not reproduced here.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\newpage\\r\\n\\\\milestoneheader{2}{Learning Models for Pressure Forecasting}{4/5}\\r\\n\\\\sectiontitle{3. Your Anticipated Contributions}\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.5mm}\\\\labeltext{3.1 Anticipated contribution relative to prior work}\\\\par\\\\vspace{.7mm}\\r\\nWe propose Gradient Boosting (GradientBoostingRegressor) for recorded n1(+15) using the six local signals. It offers a course-level alternative to the graph/recurrent and adaptive-kNN predictors above. Boosting already classifies L-Town leakage regions [\\\\hyperlink{ref:13}{13},\\\\hyperlink{ref:14}{14}]; Artesia estimates night pressure with ExtraTrees but specifies no future lead [\\\\hyperlink{ref:15}{15}], pp. 1-2. Our review did not identify this exact six-signal n1(+15) experiment. The expected contribution is a reproducible comparison on the released SCADA, including operating/leak conditions. It would test a useful extension of the reviewed solutions; it is not a new algorithm or a claim of worldwide priority.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n{\\\\footnotesize\\\\setlength{\\\\tabcolsep}{4pt}\\\\renewcommand{\\\\arraystretch}{1.16}\\r\\n\\\\rowcolors{2}{PaleBlue}{white}\\r\\n\\\\begin{tabularx}{\\\\linewidth}{@{}>{\\\\hsize=0.57540\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X>{\\\\hsize=1.42460\\\\hsize\\\\raggedright\\\\arraybackslash}X@{}}\\r\\n\\\\rowcolor{Blue!13} \\\\textbf{Planned output} & \\\\textbf{What we expect to learn} \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}C1. Forecast-ready table} & Which past inputs belong to each +15 label; reproducible time-based splits. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}C2. Boosted-tree forecast} & Whether boosting reduces error relative to persistence, Ridge and Random Forest. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\textbf{\\\\color{Navy}C3. Auxiliary-signal assessment} & Whether tank, pump, peers and local demand add value beyond n1 history. \\\\\\\\\\r\\n\\\\end{tabularx}\\\\par}\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.5mm}\\\\labeltext{3.2 Preparation, learning models and course scope}\\\\par\\\\vspace{.7mm}\\r\\nUse 12 readings per signal: current, 5 min earlier, ..., 55 min earlier. These give 72 columns. Add sine/cosine of clock time and seven weekday indicators: 81 inputs, or 21 inputs for n1-only. These are derived columns, not extra sensors. Align timestamps/check units and missing values; set y to n1 three timestamps later. Remove incomplete rows and labels belonging to the next split. Fit Ridge scaling using training data only; leak labels stay outside X. Section 1 EDA motivates the baselines but does not prove that all six signals help.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\[\\\\widehat p_{n1}(u+15)=F_M(X(u)),\\\\qquad F_m(X)=F_{m-1}(X)+\\\\eta h_m(X).\\\\]\\r\\nBoosting starts with the training-target mean and adds shallow trees that learn the remaining prediction errors; eta controls each correction [\\\\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. The target remains absolute future pressure. This is the sequential error-correction idea of Module 11.6/Week 7. Ridge is a linear model with a penalty on large coefficients [\\\\hyperlink{ref:16}{16}]. Random Forest averages trees fitted to resampled training rows [\\\\hyperlink{ref:17}{17}]. Boosted trees can capture nonlinear relationships among pressure, tank storage, pump operation and local consumption; an advantage over these baselines must be tested.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\nInitial model settings, to be checked on validation data: boosting stages \\\\{100,200,300\\\\} Ã— learning rates \\\\{0.03,0.1\\\\}, depth=2, minimum leaf=20, squared-error loss, subsample=1; no random-split early stopping. Forest: 200 trees, max\\\\_features=1.0, depth \\\\{6,10,14\\\\} Ã— minimum leaf \\\\{10,40\\\\}. Ridge alpha: \\\\{0.01,0.1,1,10,100,1000\\\\}. Use tree seed 42 and six settings per learner/input set, chosen by validation MAE [\\\\hyperlink{ref:16}{16},\\\\hyperlink{ref:17}{17},\\\\hyperlink{ref:18}{18}]. These are proposed choices, not proven best settings.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\vspace{1.5mm}\\\\labeltext{3.3 Evaluation and criteria for improvement}\\\\par\\\\vspace{.7mm}\\r\\nTrain January-September 2018; choose settings October-December 2018; then refit on eligible 2018 and freeze settings before the 2019 assessment. Keep time order and the same forecast rows for every model, with 55 min of past readings. Remove labels that cross a split boundary; past inputs may come from the previous period. Since 2019 was already inspected, it is a final time-based assessment, not a blind test. Compare persistence, Ridge, Forest, full boosting and n1-only boosting. In validation, remove T1, PUMP\\\\_1, n4/n31 or AMR\\\\_n1 one group at a time, following the class attribute-removal idea.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\nRecompute MAE/RMSE on these common 2019 rows; report errors in metres and skill=1-MAE\\\\_model/MAE\\\\_persistence (positive means better than persistence). A boosting advantage requires lower MAE than persistence, Ridge and Forest; auxiliary value requires beating n1-only boosting and persistence. Limited or absent auxiliary-signal value is also informative: it supports a simpler model. Report monthly errors, RMSE tradeoffs and unsuccessful results; these comparisons do not prove statistical significance. Deliver the preparation/model notebooks, settings, score table and prediction/error plots. No learning model has been fitted. Delays/ages, balance proxies and block intervals remain outside this scope.\\\\par\\\\vspace{1.7mm}\\r\\n\\\\newpage\\r\\nrg: scripts/build_milestone*: The filename, directory name, or volume label syntax is incorrect. are estimated using a least-squares method (Montgomery 2020) on
a manually selected time period where no leaks have occurred.
LI-2: Reconstruction Error Analysis
When comparing the time series of the node pressure estimated
with the linear regression model ˆPi formulated in Eq. (12) with
the observed pressure values Pi by analyzing their difference [com-
pare Eq. (13)], additional occurring flows, i.e., leakages, contribute
to an increase of the model error. They can be regarded in Eq. (12)
as part of additional demand in the WDN. This yields the following
definition of the MRE:

value of 0 for all time steps tno leak. Its expectation value Eð·Þ will
be E½MREjiðtno leakÞ ¼ 0.
The pressure drop ΔP ¼ PðtleakÞ −Pðtno leakÞ caused at a certain
location in the WDN by a leakage can be calculated as the difference
of the pressure value observed at that location in the two time steps
before (tno leak) and after (tleak) the leak start. Considering a leakage
ls occurring at a location with corresponding MAS s, the pressure
drop at s due to the leakage will be greater in magnitude than for all
other sensor locations. Because the pressure drop is negative in sign,
the following relation is obtained:
ΔPs < ΔPi;

When combining Eqs. (13) and (14) and choosing s ¼ j, the

Furthermore, let the vector MREs be represented by the MRE
for the combination of node s with all nodes i; then the magnitude
of that vector may be calculated with the Frobenius-norm k · kF
(cf. Golub and Van Loan 2013). Finally, the detection of a single
new leak occurring in the WDN, along with its starting time, may
be accomplished by applying change point detection (Step LI-3) to
the time series kMREsðtÞkF. After a leak is definitively identified
and becomes distinct, the model may be recalibrated to be reset for
new leakage identification.
Multiple simultaneous leaks will also be reflected in the time
series kMREsðtÞkF in an aggregated form [compare Eq. (13)].
However, disaggregation into individual leaks is not straightfor-
ward in the current implementation of LILA. Thus, for a more pre-
cise analysis of each individual leak within the overall aggregated
error, we manually selected, for each leak, an individual combina-
tion of nodes that revealed the leak signal in the most distinct
fashion.
LI-3: Change Point Detection
The last task of leakage identification is the detection of variations
in the MRE time series induced by a leak flow; therefore, this rep-
resents a change point detection problem. Change point detection
problems have been widely studied in the literature, and there is a
multitude of statistical methods available. For a comprehensive
review we refer the reader to Aminikhanghahi and Cook (2017).
As explained in the previous section, we expect the MRE to be
unbiased and normally distributed for the network state without
leaks. With the start of a leak event, the error mean will either
abruptly or gradually shift away from zero, depending on the nature
of the leak. Successful change point detection aims to flag these
instances as early as possible, while at the same time avoiding false
alarms. In LILA, we apply the widely used Cusum algorithm
(Montgomery 2020), known from statistical process monitoring,
to automatically detect changes in the MRE.
The Cusum algorithm is a method to detect changes in the prob-
ability distribution of an univariate signal. It accumulates the dis-
tance Ci of the target variable x from its mean μx, corrected by a
slack value K, which is chosen based on the change of mean that is
to be detected. Often K is selected based on the estimated standard
deviation σx of the target variable multiplied by a parameter δ
[cf. Eq. (16)]. A change point is flagged once Ci exceeds a prede-
fined threshold H ¼ η × σx. In general, the algorithm comes in two
forms for detecting positive and negative changes from the mean.
For our application, we use the formulation to detect changes in the
positive direction because leaks will manifest themselves as posi-
tive values in the MRE (cf. section LI-2: Reconstruction Error
Analysis)
Cþ
i ¼ max½0; xi −ðμ0 þ KÞ þ Cþ
i−1
with
Cþ
0 ¼ 0
and
K ¼ δ
2 · σx
ð16Þ
To evaluate the general suitability of the Cusum method for the
leak identification problem, we created artificial error trajectories
by adding different levels of white noise to given ground truth leak
trajectories. This allowed us to confirm the algorithm’s ability to
robustly flag the leaks and to select the hyperparameters in a rea-
sonable range (cf. Appendix). Moreover, we can derive some useful
intuitions. Naturally, the change point method performs better for
higher ratios of leak magnitude to signal noise. This reflects the fact
that a smaller model error and better signal quality enable earlier
leak detection. The same holds for the temporal development of the
leak, as abrupt bursts are easier to detect than slow transients.
Leakage Localization
The leakage localization step has three subcomponents, including
water demand calibration, pressure difference analysis to identify
the pipes with the highest influence on the pressure sensors, and the
application of the SLA method developed by Berglund et al. (2017)
to quantify the approximate leakage magnitude and localize the
leak in the WDN. The three steps shown in Fig. 1 work sequentially
to, first, partially calibrate the hydraulic model using demand data.
Second, a pressure difference analysis is conducted to define the
order of the pipes that affect each pressure sensor of the water net-
work and report them as leak candidates. Third, the application of
SLA aims at finding the magnitude of a leak from the candidates
that could be producing the discrepancy between nonleak and leak
pressure values. A detailed explanation of each leak localization
component is presented in what follows.
LL-1: Water Demand Calibration
Water demand calibration aims at matching the simulated and the
observed demand to replicate real conditions with the hydraulic
model (Walski 2017). LILA requires a demand-calibrated model
to replicate the demand and the pressure measured values reported
by the sensors. To calibrate the demand patterns and base demand
in the WDN hydraulic model, we use automatic meter reading
(AMR) data available for the simulation period. The new calibrated

1) * np.square(self.STMerror) + np.square(self.STMpredict(x) - y)) / STMsize)\\r\\n255:                 LTMsize = len(self.LTMX)\\r\\n256:                 self.LTMerror = np.sqrt(((LTMsize - 1) * np.square(self.LTMerror) + np.square(self.LTMpredict(x) - y)) / LTMsize)\\r\\n257:                 size = STMsize + LTMsize\\r\\n258:                 self.COMBerror = np.sqrt(((size - 1) * np.square(self.COMBerror) + np.square(self.COMBpredict(x) - y)) / size)\\r\\n259:             else:\\r\\n260:                 self.modelError = np.sqrt(((self.sampleCount - 1) * np.square(self.modelError) + np.square(np.sum(self.predict([x]) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / self.sampleCount)\\r\\n261:                 STMsize = len(self.STMX)\\r\\n262:                 self.STMerror = np.sqrt(((STMsize - 1) * np.square(self.STMerror) + np.square(np.sum(self.STMpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / STMsize)\\r\\n263:                 LTMsize = len(self.LTMX)\\r\\n264:                 self.LTMerror = np.sqrt(((LTMsize - 1) * np.square(self.LTMerror) + np.square(np.sum(self.LTMpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / LTMsize)\\r\\n265:                 size = STMsize + LTMsize\\r\\n266:                 self.COMBerror = np.sqrt(((size - 1) * np.square(self.COMBerror) + np.square(np.sum(self.COMBpredict(x) - y)) / y.shape[0]) / size)\\r\\n349:         mem_list = [self.STMerror, self.LTMerror, self.COMBerror]\\r\\n350:         best_mem_ind = np.argmin(mem_list)\\r\\n351:         if (best_mem_ind != self.best_mem):\\r\\n352:             self.best_mem = best_mem_ind\\r\\n353:         if (best_mem_ind == 0):\\r\\n354:             return np.array([self.STMpredict(x) for x in X])\\r\\n355:         elif (best_mem_ind == 1):\\r\\n356:             return np.array([self.LTMpredict(x) for x in X])\\r\\n357:         elif (best_mem_ind == 2):\\r\\n358:             return np.array([self.COMBpredict(x) for x in X])\\r\\n\"}"

### aggregated_output

````text
106:scope: Apply controlled revisions to the Milestone 2 PDF while preserving prior artifacts and style, then validate exact feature, layout, file-integrity, and portal constraints.
121:- when revising an academic report, the user required targeted changes without losing prior versions or the established visual style -> preserve existing artifacts and make controlled incremental revisions. [Task 1]
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python

\labeltext{Area of interest and selection rationale.}
My research interests include pressure management in drinking-water distribution systems, particularly pressure behavior at remote critical nodes. I have previously worked on this problem using field measurements and model-based control methods, as described in a \href{https://ascelibrary.org/doi/10.1061/JWRMD5.WRENG-7321}{\textit{published case study}}. For this mini-project, I would like to study whether supervised machine learning can use current and historical SCADA measurements to forecast future pressure at a monitored node. The assignment asks for Kaggle datasets with usability above 9. BATADAL satisfies that criterion with a Kaggle usability of 10.0. BattLeDIM currently shows 3.53 on Kaggle, so it does not meet that specific threshold; I retain it as a candidate because its original source has substantial academic support, including a peer-reviewed ASCE benchmark paper, an official research archive, and EPANET network models.
\end{contextbox}

## 2026-10-19T01:09:48.846Z · source line 8595 · Assistant message · AgentMessage

Source: `original_sessions/rollout-2026-10-11T10-33-18-01a12b61-e72c-7da1-bb50-45bff9d85d03.jsonl#L8595`  
Event ID: `msg_02d303a6a82c3a1a016ad56dd44b7081a28e2b2938c2d3d4a7`  
Turn ID: `01a151b4-c46a-7511-9623-f065863d680b`

### Message

```text
Ese párrafo es un **glosario para interpretar los resultados de detección de fugas** de los artículos revisados.

| Sigla | Significado | Explicación sencilla |
|---|---|---|
| **TP** | *True Positives*: verdaderos positivos | Fugas reales identificadas correctamente, según los criterios de evaluación del estudio. |
| **FP** | *False Positives*: falsos positivos | Alarmas falsas o localizaciones incorrectas, según cómo las contabilice cada estudio. |
| **FN** | *False Negatives*: falsos negativos | Fugas reales que el método no detectó. |
| **TPR** | *True Positive Rate*, también llamado **recall** o sensibilidad | Proporción de fugas reales detectadas: **TP / (TP + FN)**. La sigla del documento es TPR. |
| **F1** | Medida que combina **precision** y **recall** | Resume el equilibrio entre detectar fugas y evitar falsas alarmas. Va de **0 a 1**; un valor mayor indica mejor equilibrio. |
| **CUSUM** | *Cumulative Sum*: suma acumulada | Método estadístico que acumula desviaciones respecto a un comportamiento de referencia para detectar cambios persistentes. En LILA ayuda a identificar señales de fuga. |

Para entender **F1**, primero conviene distinguir:

- **Precision:** de todas las alarmas emitidas, ¿cuántas fueron correctas? → **TP / (TP + FP)**.
- **Recall:** de todas las fugas reales, ¿cuántas se detectaron? → **TP / (TP + FN)**.
- **F1:** combina ambas mediante **2 × precision × recall / (precision + recall)**.

**CUSUM**, por ejemplo, puede acumular pequeñas diferencias entre la presión esperada y la observada. Si esas diferencias persisten y superan un umbral, genera una señal de cambio.

**EUR** es la puntuación monetaria de la competencia, expresada en euros. Y **“Training was not reproduced”** significa que revisamos los métodos y sus resultados publicados, pero no repetimos su entrenamiento.

Estas métricas describen principalmente **detección de fugas**. Para nuestro pronóstico de presión de **n1 a +15 minutos**, evaluaremos el error con **MAE y RMSE**, expresados en metros.
```

import collections,hashlib,json,re,shutil,subprocess,sys

OUT=W/'output/pdf/milestone2-v028'
OLD=BASE/'Milestone2_Corrected.pdf'

106:scope: Apply controlled revisions to the Milestone 2 PDF while preserving prior artifacts and style, then validate exact feature, layout, file-integrity, and portal constraints.
121:- when revising an academic report, the user required targeted changes without losing prior versions or the established visual style -> preserve existing artifacts and make controlled incremental revisions. [Task 1]
---
name: "pdf"
description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.

## Final response

### Final Response Citations

- **Create/edit:** for created or edited PDFs, cite each final deliverable exactly once with `purpose="output"`, in its own paragraph. Briefly describe the result without repeating its filename, path, or link. Never put output directives inside prose or list items.
- **Q&A and summaries:** Every file whose contents you summarize or use to support an answer MUST receive a `:codex-file-citation{...}` with `purpose="source"` in the final response. Use the source citation in place of the filename, wherever the filename would appear. Do not write the filename separately and append its citation at the end. Files merely inspected but not used in the answer need no citation. Do not emit output citations for unchanged files or read-only answers.

- Emit `:codex-file-citation{...}` directly, without backticks, code fences, or a Markdown-link wrapper. Use verified targets only. No need to cite or reference intermediate work.

For file citations, set `path` to the verified absolute file path on the task host. Omit `mode`.

Some illustrative examples, real responses use verified targets outside code fences.

```text
:codex-file-citation{path="/absolute/path/review.pdf" purpose="output"}
```

```text
The review of :codex-file-citation{path="/absolute/path/review.pdf" purpose="source"} is due Friday.
```


### Additional Citation Guidance

For PDF Q&A, inspect complete relevant pages and preserve material headings, table/figure labels, footnotes, sources, and sample sizes. PDF citations support only plain file citations; do not add `artifact_kind`, `page_number`, or other locators.

description: "Read, create, inspect, render, and verify PDF files where visual layout matters, including fillable AcroForms. Use Poppler rendering plus Python tools such as reportlab, pdfplumber, and pypdf for generation and extraction."
---

# PDF Skill

## When To Use

- Read or review PDF content where layout and visuals matter.
- Create PDFs programmatically with reliable formatting.
- Fill and validate interactive PDF forms.
- Validate final rendering before delivery.

## Tools + Contract Requirements

Immediately before the first create/edit authoring command, run `mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs` successfully exactly once using the command below. Do not run it for read-only work. For edits, replace `create` with `edit`; adjust the expected count and output format to match the requested outputs.



```bash
node container_tools/mark_artifact_operation_started.mjs --operation-kind create --expected-output-count 1 --output-format pdf
```

## Workflow

1. Prefer visual review: render PDF pages to PNGs and inspect them.
   - Use `pdftoppm` from the bundled runtime or system Poppler when available.
   - If unavailable, install Poppler or ask the user to review the output locally.
2. Use `reportlab` to generate PDFs when creating new documents.
3. Use `pdfplumber` or `pypdf` for text extraction and quick checks; do not rely on text extraction for layout fidelity.
4. After each meaningful update, re-render pages and verify alignment, spacing, and legibility.

## Fill And Validate AcroForms

Visual review alone is not a correctness check for a fillable PDF. A page `/Widget` annotation can render a value from its appearance stream while the canonical `/AcroForm/Fields` tree is missing or contains a stale value.

1. Keep the result interactive by default; set `flatten=True` only when the user explicitly requests a completed, static form. Preserve the source PDF, and do not flatten a signed PDF without an explicit workflow decision.
2. Inspect both representations before filling: enumerate fields from `reader.get_fields()` and `/Widget` annotations from every page's `/Annots`, following `/Parent` and `/Kids`. If a widget and a canonical field have the same name but are distinct objects with no `/Parent` relationship, do not call `reattach_fields()` blindly: it can create a second top-level field with the same name. Report the ambiguity or produce a static result.
3. Recover genuinely orphaned widgets, fill all pages, and write the result with `pypdf`:

```python
from pypdf import PdfReader, PdfWriter
from pypdf.generic import NameObject

reader = PdfReader(input_pdf)
writer = PdfWriter()
writer.clone_document_from_reader(reader)

# Restores widgets that are missing from /AcroForm/Fields.
writer.reattach_fields()
fields = writer.get_fields() or {}
missing = set(expected_values) - set(fields)
if missing:
    raise ValueError(f"Form fields not found after repair: {sorted(missing)}")

values_to_write = dict(expected_values)
if flatten:
    # Paint every existing value before removing every widget.
    values_to_write = {
        name: field.get("/V", "/Off" if field.get("/FT") == "/Btn" else "")
        for name, field in fields.items()
    }
    values_to_write.update(expected_values)

writer.update_page_form_field_values(
    None, values_to_write, auto_regenerate=False, flatten=flatten
)

if flatten:
    # pypdf's flatten=True paints appearances but does not remove widgets.
    writer.remove_annotations(subtypes="/Widget")
    writer.root_object.pop(NameObject("/AcroForm"), None)

with open(output_pdf, "wb") as stream:
    writer.write(stream)
```

4. Reopen the written PDF before delivery. For an interactive result, require every expected field to be present in `get_fields()` with the expected `/V`, enumerate page widgets again, and confirm their effective `/V` (the widget value or inherited `/Parent` value) agrees. Confirm each updated widget has a non-empty `/AP` `/N` appearance and render the final pages to catch stale or clipped appearances. Do not rely on `/NeedAppearances` or a successful PNG render as proof that logical field data was updated.
5. For a flattened result, require zero `/Widget` annotations and no remaining `/AcroForm` field tree after reopening, then render the final pages. Keep an editable copy when the user may need to revise the form.

## Temp And Output Conventions

- Use `tmp/pdfs/` for intermediate files; delete them when done.
- Write final artifacts under `output/pdf/` when working in this repo.
- Keep filenames stable and descriptive.

## Dependencies

Prefer the Codex bundled workspace/runtime dependencies when available. The primary runtime is expected to include:

- Python packages: `reportlab`, `pdfplumber`, `pypdf`
- Rendering tools: `pdftoppm` and `pdfinfo` from Poppler

If a dependency is missing, install only what is needed.

Python packages:

```bash
uv pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

If `uv` is unavailable:

```bash
python3 -m pip install reportlab pdfplumber pypdf
```

System tools for rendering:

```bash
# macOS (Homebrew)
brew install poppler

# Ubuntu/Debian
sudo apt-get install -y poppler-utils
```

If installation is not possible in this environment, tell the user which dependency is missing and how to install it locally.

## Environment

No required environment variables.

## Rendering Command

```bash
pdftoppm -png "$INPUT_PDF" "$OUTPUT_PREFIX"
```

## Quality Expectations

- Maintain polished visual design: consistent typography, spacing, margins, and section hierarchy.
- Avoid rendering issues: clipped text, overlapping elements, broken tables, black squares, or unreadable glyphs.
- Charts, tables, and images must be sharp, aligned, and clearly labeled.
- Use ASCII hyphens only. Avoid U+2011 and other Unicode dashes.
- Citations and references must be human-readable; never leave tool tokens or placeholder strings.

## Final Checks

- Do not deliver until the latest PNG inspection shows zero visual or formatting defects.
- Confirm headers, footers, page numbering, and section transitions look polished.
- Keep intermediate files organized or remove them after final approval.
